| Detox Scalp Scrub | cosmetic | salicylic acid (cosmetic use) not stated | scalp scrub | women | US cosmetic. No prescription. | Unknown today; $44 one-time for a 2-pack in a 2023 review | Bought directly, no consultation. Not part of the quiz result as far as reviewers describe it. The help centre says non-prescription products can be sold in a kit with a prescription product after a consultation. | inferred |

## Who gets what
- Baseline woman: 38, widening parting, gradual over 2 years, not pregnant, not breastfeeding, not planning a pregnancy, periods regular, no conditions, no medicines → Oral Minoxidil; Biotin + Minoxidil Chew; Biotin + Minoxidil Gummy; Hair Vitamins + Minoxidil; Hair Blends Serum (also called Hair Blends Solution) [inferred] Not observed (site blocked). Expected from Hers's documents: one or two minoxidil-based prescription products (NCOA, Nov 2026: 'one or two products will be recommended'), most likely Oral Minoxidil or a Hair Blends product, swappable. Finasteride products are excluded because she is not past menopau
- Woman past menopause with gradual thinning, no conditions, no medicines → Hair Blends Postmenopause Serum (Postmeno Serum); Postmenopausal Hair Loss Spray (finasteride + minoxidil); Oral Minoxidil; Biotin + Minoxidil Chew; Biotin + Minoxidil Gummy; Hair Vitamins + Minoxidil; Hair Blends Serum (also called Hair Blends Solution) [docs] Hers says finasteride is offered to eligible women who have gone through menopause, as a finasteride plus minoxidil topical (Postmeno Serum; earlier a spray). The minoxidil-only products remain possible. Not observed. Stage 3: the spray (p7) may have been replaced by the serum (p6).
- Woman who is pregnant or planning a pregnancy → nothing [inferred] Hers's safety text rules out every tablet and finasteride. No source says what, if anything, the quiz then offers; over-the-counter products can still be bought directly but Hers does not recommend them for pregnancy in any text found. Not observed. Stage 3 label change (docs to inferred): the exclu
- Woman who is breastfeeding → Hair Blends Serum (also called Hair Blends Solution); Minoxidil 5% Foam; Minoxidil 2% Solution (drops) [inferred] Tablets are ruled out. Hers's FAQ is summarised by the search index as saying some topical solutions are possible while breastfeeding, without naming them, so the three topicals listed here are the candidates, not a confirmed offer. Not observed.
- Woman with heart disease, a blood pressure problem, or kidney or liver disease → Hair Blends Serum (also called Hair Blends Solution); Minoxidil 5% Foam; Minoxidil 2% Solution (drops) [inferred] Oral minoxidil 'may not be appropriate'; the provider decides. A topical is the likely alternative. Spironolactone is itself a heart and blood pressure medicine with its own exclusions, so it is unlikely here. Inferred from Hers's safety text; not observed.
- Woman with sudden shedding or patchy loss (not female pattern hair loss) → nothing [inferred] Unknown. A sentence saying Hers does not offer oral minoxidil for telogen effluvium or alopecia areata was found only on a non-public copy of the product page (not confirmed). The 2026 review that says women with sudden, patchy or severe loss should see a healthcare provider is about the over-the-co
- Woman who does not want a prescription → Minoxidil 5% Foam; Minoxidil 2% Solution (drops); Biotin Gummies (Biotin Builder); Triple Threat Shampoo and Conditioner; Full Volume (Volumizing) Shampoo and Conditioner [docs] Bought directly from product pages with no quiz (help centre: non-prescription products 'do not necessitate a medical consultation'; Healthline: guest checkout). Hers does not state a default; the 5% foam once a day is the usual first choice for women (our reading).
- Baseline man: 32, receding hairline and thinning crown → nothing [inferred] Not served. Hers is the women's brand; men are served by the sister site Hims. No Hers men's flow exists to test.

## Strengths
- Many formats of one proven drug: plain oral minoxidil, three minoxidil plus vitamin formats (pill, chew, gummy) and a topical serum, so almost any preference can be met without changing the active ingredient.
- A clear, defensible finasteride rule (after menopause only), with a dedicated postmenopausal product.
- Low entry price: $29 a month for oral minoxidil and over-the-counter minoxidil from $12.67 a month on a long plan.
- The patient can swap the suggested treatment before the provider sees it (reported), and pays nothing until a treatment is selected.
- Written patient information for every prescription product is public in the help centre, and compounded products are clearly labelled as not FDA-approved.
- Narrow scope: Hers is reported to say it does not offer oral minoxidil for telogen effluvium or alopecia areata (sentence found only on a non-public copy of the page; not confirmed).

## Weaknesses
- The quiz result sits behind an account (reported), so a visitor cannot see a recommendation or a final price without giving contact details.
- The consultation is reported as thin on cause-finding: no reviewer mentions questions on thyroid disease, iron or anaemia, PCOS or signs of high androgens, recent illness or weight loss, scalp symptoms, family history, contraception or period regularity. No blood tests and no blood pressure reading are described, although the main drug is a blood pressure medicine.
- Most prescription products are compounded and not FDA-approved, and the strengths of the oral blends are not published on the pages found.
- Eligibility rules are scattered: exclusion lists sit on separate treatment terms pages (/treatment/hair-loss/...), apart from the product pages (pages not opened; from search-index reads).
- No source shows dutasteride, oral finasteride for women, tretinoin add-ons or iron or vitamin D testing in the range. Spironolactone, the usual anti-androgen tablet for women before menopause, appears in no 2023 to 2026 description of the hair range, and one 2026 reviewer says Hers does not offer it.
- Prices are hard to pin down: sources give $35 and $39 for the blends, $39 and $50 a month for the postmenopausal spray, and plan-length prices are only shown inside the flow.
- Reviewers are said to report complaints about subscription charges, refunds and reaching a clinician (stage 1 reading; not re-checked at stage 3).

## Opportunities
- Show the recommendation, strength and full price before asking for an email or an account.
- Ask the cause-finding questions Hers is not reported to ask (thyroid, iron, PCOS signs, recent illness, scalp symptoms, medicines that cause shedding) and explain how each answer changed the plan.
- Offer a route for the women Hers's stated policy leaves out: sudden shedding, patchy loss, pregnancy and breastfeeding (advice, safe topicals, blood tests, referral).
- Ask for a home blood pressure and pulse reading before oral minoxidil, and say so up front. If offering spironolactone (Hers appears not to today), include a potassium check plan.
- Publish who cannot have each treatment on the product page itself, and state exact strengths for combination products.
- Give perimenopausal women a clear answer: Hers's finasteride rule is 'after menopause only', with nothing specific in between.
- Sell fewer, simpler options with fixed, visible plan prices; Hers's many near-identical minoxidil formats are hard to compare.

## Verification verdict
Trust this report as a map of Hers's published policy and product range, not as a description of its quiz. The site was blocked at all three stages, so no answer was ever changed and replayed and nothing is observed. The safety rules that rest on the help-centre leaflets (finasteride not before menopause; no oral minoxidil in pregnancy, when planning one or when breastfeeding; pheochromocytoma; heart history to the provider; provider decides; video visit in some states) were re-read word for word and are solid as policy. The product list is broadly right for the five core prescription products and the over-the-counter items, with prices good to within a few dollars and several strengths unknown. The quiz outline is second-hand, partly three years old, and three of its questions have no traceable source. Spironolactone and the spray may no longer be sold. After the stage 3 corrections the labels are honest: 'docs' means Hers, its help centre or a partner article says so (sometimes read only through a search index, which is flagged each time); 'inferred' covers reviewer reports and reasoning. A reader should not use this file to say what any single answer does in the Hers quiz.

## Limitations
- Nothing was observed live on Hers at either stage: the site is behind a security checkpoint that was not, and must not be, defeated. No answer was changed and replayed, so there are no tested rules.
- Every rule is Hers's stated eligibility or safety policy (docs) or reasoning from it (inferred). The documents say who should not have a product; they do not say whether the quiz blocks the patient, hides the product, shows a warning or leaves it to the provider.
- Question wording is not verbatim and most answer options are unknown. Options shown as 'implied' or 'reviewer's category' are not the site's wording.
- Not tested, because the quiz could not be reached: age bands, each hair pattern, speed and duration, scalp symptoms, family history, each medical condition, each medicine, allergies, previous treatments and side effects, treatment preference, goals, lifestyle questions, and entry from product pages against the main page.
- Whether the health questions come before or after the account step is unresolved (a 2023 review says before; a 2026 article implies after).
- The conditions half of the spironolactone 'not a candidate' list, and the exact safety wording on product pages, were read only as search-index summaries and may be incomplete or out of date.
- Prices come from a search index and from reviews of different dates and disagree in places; plan-length prices for prescription products were not found.
- The default product for any profile is unknown; the 'offered' lists in recommendations are the products the patient is eligible for on paper, not a seen result.
- The quiz definition (probably a code chunk named hers.us.modern.domains-consultation-hair-loss.<hash>.js) was not read; reading it would settle most of the above.
- Stage 3 re-read the third-party reviews through a fetch tool that summarises pages, so short quotes from reviews are close to, but not guaranteed to be, word for word. Help-centre quotes were checked against the saved page text and are exact.
- Stage 3 could not re-read Hers's own pages: the site is blocked and the shared web-search budget ran out after three searches. Statements marked 'via search index' are therefore still second-hand.
- Whether spironolactone and the postmenopausal spray are still sold is not settled.
- Seen only in a 2023 review and left out of the catalogue: Complete Hair Kit ($55), Weekender Kit ($40), collagen powder ($27) and Latisse for eyelashes.


---

# Hims UK (with Hers UK for women) (UK) — digest
Slug: hims-uk · Verified: yes · Audiences: men, women

Model: Subscription telehealth: a long marketing quiz, a medical questionnaire reviewed by a UK clinician without a call, then compounded finasteride and minoxidil products (sprays, serum, chews) or generics posted every 1 to 12 months, paid up front.
Account wall: Men (Hims): after 21 anonymous screens, at https://app.forhims.co.uk/start/signup?t=hair&anon_flow_key=hair — 'Ready to see if you’re eligible? Next we'll cover your health and medical history as well as treatment options.' asks for an email address. Nothing medical, no product and no price has been shown by then; the last thing before the wall is a fixed 'Great news—we have options that may work for you', an answer summary and marketing screens. Women (Hers): at the first screen (first and last name).
Tested by: live-walk, site-code, public-docs
Coverage: Stage 1 (map) walked the whole anonymous men's quiz and read the served quiz definition, product and price tables, app code and public documents. Stage 2 (experiment) replayed the baseline twice and then changed one answer at a time: 31 runs covering every non-baseline option of all 11 anonymous questions, so all 42 options have now been clicked live. Seven further runs checked two multi-select combinations, the phone layout, a returning visitor, skipping the quiz, two other landing pages and the Hers entry: 40 replays in all. Every run kept the full server traffic, which showed the server choosing the next step after each answer and returning no result at the end. Stage 2 also read ten more of the app's code files (how the server's eligibility result is used, the consent screen, brand settings) and the older Hims UK help centre (vitamin strengths in the chews). Not obtained, and not obtainable without an account: the wording of the medical questions, the rules that map answers to products, any live recommendation, and the checkout prices. Nothing at all could be tested on Hers UK, which asks for a name first. Stage 3 (verify) added 25 reviewer replays in runs/verify-* (two baselines, a full-trace baseline, six repeated one-change runs, and 16 runs on behaviour stage 2 had not tested or had tested once), re-read 16 public pages, re-read the served code behind every code-based rule, and re-read the leaflet and help-centre lines behind every document-based rule. The wall is where stage 1 and stage 2 said it is, and nothing behind it could be tested.

Questions: 53 (25 before sign-in; 11 branch). Flows: men (37q), men-product-link (0q), generic-entry (1q), men-reactivation (1q), women (14q)

## Engine summary
Hims UK's engine has two parts, and only the first can be watched from outside. Part one is a marketing quiz anyone can take: 11 questions (intent, where, how much, when, goals, hair type, hair length, family history, stress, styling, time) with 10 selling screens between them. Stage 2 changed every answer one at a time (31 replays covering all 42 options) and the engine did not move: same screens, same order, same 'Great news—we have options that may work for you', same email wall. Six answers are repeated on one summary screen; the other five are never shown again. No answer stops anyone, warns anyone, or shows a product, strength or price. The server traffic agrees: after each answer the server names the next step, and at the end it returns a record id and nothing else. If the visitor signs up, his stored answers are attached to the new account. Part two sits behind the account: date of birth, a medical questionnaire, then a server-side rules step that marks each of ten products eligible or not and names one as the recommendation. A patient the server calls 'not eligible' sees a rejection screen. Everyone else sees one recommended product, with the other nine a tap away and the ruled-out ones greyed out as 'Not available based on your medical history'. The 'Selected based on your goals' chips on each product page are fixed text, not built from his answers. He then picks a plan length, ticks a consent box, gives GP details and pays up front; a clinician reviews the answers without a call and approves or refunds. The wording of the medical questions and the answer-to-product rules are not public. The best guides to them are Hims's own leaflets (who must not use each product) and the old platform's tag system, in which an answer could exclude every product tagged 'finasteride', 'minoxidil-oral', 'topical' and so on. Hers UK runs on the same platform with five minoxidil-based treatments for women, but asks for a name on the first screen, so nothing there can be tested without an account. Stage 3 (independent check): the reviewer replayed the baseline from fresh browsers and got the recorded end state, repeated six one-change runs with the same result, and tried what stage 2 had not: contradictory answers, every warning-sign answer together, going back, leaving and returning, deleting answers, a product code followed to the wall. None of it moves the engine before the wall. Everything said about the engine after the wall is still read from code, leaflets and help pages, or reasoned, and none of it was seen happening.

## Rules
| id | when | then | effect | evidence | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | any one anonymous answer is changed (each of the 31 non-baseline options of men-q1 to men-q11, one at a time) | Nothing in the engine changes before the wall: the same 21 screens in the same order, the same server-chosen next step after every answer, the same 'Great news—we have options that may work for you', and the same email wall. No answer stops the visitor, adds or removes a question, shows a warning, or shows or hides a product, strength, plan or price. | none | observed | high |
| R20 | men-q2 (where), men-q4 (when), men-q8 (family history), men-q9 (stress), men-q10 (styling) or men-q11 (time) is changed | One line of the screen 'Here’s what we know about your hair so far' repeats the new answer. That is the only visible use of any answer in the anonymous quiz. | marketing-only | observed | high |
| R21 | men-q1 (intent), men-q3 (how much hair lost), men-q5 (results hoped for), men-q6 (hair type) or men-q7 (hair length) is changed | No visible difference on any screen. The answer is posted to the server and never shown back to the visitor. | none | observed | high |
| R22 | a 'warning sign' answer is given: 'No hair loss yet', 'None of the above', loss 'All over', started 'In the past few months', 'I don’t have hair', 'Buzzed, shaved, or bald', family history 'No' or 'Not sure', stress 'All the time' | No warning, no extra question and no stop: the visitor still sees 'Great news—we have options that may work for you' and the sign-up screen. Hims's own leaflets exclude sudden hair loss and total baldness, and three topical leaflets exclude a shaved scalp, so any screening for these can only happen after sign-up. The reviewer also gave every warning-sign answer in one run, and a set of answers that contradict each other ('No hair loss yet' with 'A lot' of hair lost; 'I don’t have hair' with hair length 'Long'): same result, so the quiz has no check that the answers fit together. | none | observed | high |
| R23 | men-q5 (results hoped for): no box ticked, 'All of the above', or several boxes | Continue stays greyed out until one box is ticked. 'All of the above' must stand alone: ticking it clears any other tick, and it is sent as the single value ALL. Two ordinary ticks are sent as a list of two. None of these changes any later screen. Reviewer's extra checks (stage 3): unticking the only ticked box greys Continue out again; ticking an ordinary box after 'All of the above' clears 'All of the above'; all four ordinary boxes ticked by hand are sent as a list of four values, not as ALL, and the 'All of the above' box stays unticked. The next screen and everything after it are the same in each case. | none | observed | high |
| R24 | the last anonymous screen is reached | After 5 seconds the browser tells the server the quiz is finished. The server's reply holds only a record id: no eligibility result, no product and no price. The browser then opens the sign-up page, which asks for an email address. | none | observed | high |

| R26 | a visitor who has finished the quiz opens the quiz address again in the same browser | He is sent straight back to the sign-up email screen; the quiz does not start again. That screen has no 'Your answers' menu (the menu with 'Delete my answers' is only on the quiz screens, rule R41), and its back arrow leads to the Hims home page, not back into the quiz. So once he has finished, no way to retake the quiz or to delete his answers was found in that browser. | skip-questions | observed | high |
| R27 | the quiz is taken on a phone-sized screen, or started from another landing page or a product page | Same quiz. The phone layout shows the same 21 screens with the same wording and the same wall. Every 'Get started' button on the paid-search landing page and on the combo-kit shopping page points to /c/hl (the combo-kit button adds a product code, gst=s2hF6Mdn; the serum's code made no difference when followed in stage 1). Followed to the end by the reviewer, the combo-kit code (gst=s2hF6Mdn) is carried along in the address, including the sign-up address at the wall (/start/signup?gst=s2hF6Mdn&t=hair&anon_flow_key=hair). It changes nothing on screen: same quiz, no product and no price. It is not stored in the browser, none of the quiz's recorded requests carries it, and no reference to it was found in the app code that was read, so whether it pre-selects the product after sign-up is unknown. | none | observed | high |

So the server's rules and the clinician can see them. Nothing in the public code shows them changing a product, and the product pages do not use them. | unknown | code | medium |
| R29 | any anonymous visitor opens the quiz | He gets the same quiz version as every other visitor tested. The site asks its feature-flag service for settings under one shared visitor name ('anonymous'), so those settings cannot differ between anonymous visitors at any one moment. No sign of a per-visitor test was found: the same quiz version came back in every fresh browser, and the settings were the same for everyone (one of the 949 changed for all visitors during the evening of the check). A test run some other way, for example by the old site before the redirect, cannot be ruled out; none was seen. | none | observed | high |

With no product chosen he gets the recommendation page. This is the platform's logic for services that take payment after all questionnaires; whether Hims UK hair is set up that way was not seen. Correction on re-reading: with no product chosen, a patient who has finished every questionnaire but still has an unmet 'gate' (for example GP details) is sent to that gate first; otherwise he gets the recommendation page. ## Who gets what
- Baseline man: 32, receding hairline, some loss, began over a year ago, family history yes, wants thicker hair, no medical issues (anonymous quiz, watched live) → nothing [observed] Nothing is offered. End state in runs/exp-00-baseline-replay and runs/exp-00-baseline-full: the fixed message 'Great news—we have options that may work for you', a summary of six of his answers, four general marketing screens about finasteride and minoxidil, then 'Ready to see if you’re eligible?' w
- Every other man tested in the anonymous quiz: each of the 31 other answers one at a time, all 11 changed at once, two goals ticked, 'All of the above', and the phone layout → nothing [observed] Exactly the same end state as the baseline man in every run (runs/exp-01 to runs/exp-34-mobile, runs/map-opposite-path). Before the wall there is only one outcome. Reviewer (stage 3): 15 more men's quiz replays ended the same way (runs/verify-01-no-hair to runs/verify-20-gst-combo-kit), including co
- Man who signs up and passes the medical questionnaire (from served code, not seen live) → Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray (sold as 'The Combination Hair Loss Spray' / 'Personalised Hair Loss Sprays'; Hair Hybrids range); Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (the '4-in-1' or 'premium' spray; Hair Hybrids range); Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Serum (sold as 'The Combination Hair Loss Serum' / 'Personalised Hair Loss Serums'; Hair Hybrids range); Finasteride & Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Tablet (the 'chew'; sold as 'The Combination Treatment + Supplement Blend'; Hair Hybrids range); Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew (sold as 'The Hair Regrowth Treatment + Supplement Blend'; Hair Hybrids range); Supplement Blend chew (sold as 'Hair Loss Vitamin Supplement' / 'Personalised Hair Loss Supplements'; 'Vitamin Chewables' in the price table); Finasteride tablets (sold as 'The Hair Saving Tablets'); Alcohol-free Minoxidil Serum; Finasteride + Minoxidil Combo Kit (sold as 'The Hair Saving Tablets & Minoxidil Combo Kit'); Regaine® Liquid Solution (Regaine for Men Extra Strength Scalp Solution 5% w/v) [code] The server marks each of the ten products eligible or not and names one as the recommendation. The page shows that one first with 'Continue with this plan', and the other nine under 'Explore other treatment options', grouped Oral, Topical and Combo. Which product is named for which answers was not s
- Man who cannot have finasteride, for example already taking finasteride or dutasteride, or with a partner who is pregnant or trying (from leaflets; the engine's action was not seen) → Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew (sold as 'The Hair Regrowth Treatment + Supplement Blend'; Hair Hybrids range); Alcohol-free Minoxidil Serum; Regaine® Liquid Solution (Regaine for Men Extra Strength Scalp Solution 5% w/v); Supplement Blend chew (sold as 'Hair Loss Vitamin Supplement' / 'Personalised Hair Loss Supplements'; 'Vitamin Chewables' in the price table) [inferred] Inferred from the leaflets and the old tag system: every product containing finasteride would be greyed out, leaving the minoxidil chew, the minoxidil serum, Regaine and the supplement chew. The 2-in-1 spray and finasteride tablets give no upper age, and the supplement chew has no medicine in it. New customers over 75 are not treated at all (help centre). - Man the server rules out completely (from served code; which answers cause it is not public) → nothing [code] Rejection screen: 'This treatment may not be suitable … we’re unable to offer a Hims treatment that’s clinically appropriate under our prescribing guidelines', with 'Back home' and a contact link. No product or price is shown before sign-in, beyond 'from £13 a month' on the public pages. Reviewer (stage 3): replayed (runs/verify-baseline-women); the app's anonymous-quiz a

The others are listed under 'Select other treatment', with unsuitable ones greyed out as 'Treatment is not suit

## Strengths
- A wide compounded range: two finasteride + minoxidil sprays (one with ketoconazole and biotin), a serum, and once-daily chews that combine oral finasteride 1.1 mg, oral minoxidil 3 mg and vitamins
- A fallback for almost every exclusion: without finasteride (minoxidil chew, minoxidil serum, Regaine), without minoxidil (finasteride tablets), without any medicine (supplement chew), so few paying customers leave with nothing
- A quiz with no friction and no dead ends: one tap per question, pictures, 'Why we ask' notes, and every one of the 42 answers leads to the sign-up screen, so no lead is lost before the email is captured
- It feels personal at low cost: one summary screen repeats six answers back, and the saved answers are attached to his account if he signs up
- Low headline price on long plans (£16.50 a month for spray, serum or chew on a 6-month plan), free delivery, full refund if the clinician does not approve
- No clinician call needed for hair: the whole thing is a form, reviewed afterwards
- The regulated set-up is clearly stated: CQC-registered provider, named GPhC pharmacies, a specialist compounding pharmacy, and a consent step that says in plain words the medicine may be compounded and off-label
- Good public reviews overall: Trustpilot 4.1 from 123 reviews on the day of the check (all Hims UK services), with recent reviewers calling the questions 'easy to follow' and the company 'thorough with questions'; 4.4 from 1,913 reviews on Hims's own hair pages
- Sensible data handling in the quiz: answers are kept for 7 days so a visitor can resume where he left off, he can go back and change an answer, and 'Delete my answers' removes them from the server at once (all seen in stage 3)
- Hers UK shows real personalisation that Hims lacks: its 'Matched for you based on your goals' chips are built from the goals the woman ticked (read in code, not seen live)

## Weaknesses
- The 'free hair quiz' is not a consultation. All 42 answers were tested and none changes anything: a man with no hair loss yet, no hair at all, or loss 'all over' that began 'in the past few months' gets the same 'Great news—we have options that may work for you' as a textbook case. Hims itself says 'The quiz is not a medical assessment'
- It gives nothing back: 21 screens, then an email wall, with no result, no product, no strength and no price shown first
- What it never asks before the wall: age, sex, medical conditions, medicines, allergies, blood pressure, mood, sexual function, plans for children, earlier treatments and their side effects, scalp symptoms (itch, pain, scale, redness), patchy loss, how fast the loss came on. There is no photo step in the quiz
- The personal touch is thin: five of the eleven answers are never shown again, and the 'Selected based on your goals' line on each product page is fixed text, not the visitor's own goals
- No early warning: answers that Hims's own leaflets treat as reasons not to use a product (sudden loss, total baldness, shaved scalp) pass without comment, so those men can only find out after giving their details
- Prices are hidden until late and the cheap figure needs 6 or 12 months paid up front (for example £99 or £276); the monthly chew plan is £60. Only four of the ten products have a 'from' price on a public page (serum, minoxidil chew, finasteride tablets, combo kit). One Trustpilot reviewer ('Si', dated 19 November; the year is unclear) reports being 'Auto renewed at 43% more with no advance notice'
- Its flagship products are unlicensed compounded medicines in one fixed strength each (topical finasteride 0.3% with minoxidil 6% or 7%; a chew with finasteride 1.1 mg and minoxidil 3 mg), so one ingredient cannot be lowered or stopped without changing the whole product
- Gaps in the range: no dutasteride, no lower-strength topical finasteride, no oral minoxidil in adjustable doses, no shampoo or scalp care (its own shampoo is withdrawn), no devices, and no blood tests for causes of hair loss in the men's hair range
- The public pages do not say a photo is needed for hair (the terms only say 'You may be asked to upload photographs for assessment'), so the diagnosis seems to rest mainly on tick-boxes
- Cancelling is by message to support with a cut-off before the next order (the help centre says 48 hours, the terms say three days). Self-service cancelling is switched off in the app's settings for this brand, and changing a hair treatment plan also goes through support
- Men only on Hims; women are sent to a separate brand with a separate account and no anonymous quiz at all. Men over 65 are outside the labelled age range of seven of the ten products, and new customers over 75 are not treated
- The platform move (to Fill Function / Eucalyptus) leaves loose ends: product links whose code appears to do nothing, a dead hair subdomain, and card badges such as 'Generic Treatment' on Regaine
- Product quality complaints: three of the five newest reviews on Hims's own combo-kit page (10 to 20 November 2026) report spray pumps that stopped working
- The quiz has no check that the answers fit together: 'No hair loss yet' with 'A lot' of hair lost, or 'I don’t have hair' with hair length 'Long', pass without comment (stage 3)
- A visitor who has finished the quiz cannot retake it or delete his answers from where he lands: reopening the quiz sends him to the sign-up screen, which has no 'Delete my answers' control, and its back arrow goes to the home page (stage 3)
- Small inconsistencies on public pages: the minoxidil serum page says 'Twice-daily topical serum' and, lower down, 'Once a day drop 1 ml'; three safety-information addresses for compounded products redirect to the landing page

## Opportunities
- Give a real answer before asking for contact details: likely pattern, which treatments fit, which are ruled out and why, and the price. Hims asks 11 questions and returns nothing
- Make the quiz respond. Not one of Hims's 42 options changes anything on screen. React at once: sudden or patchy loss → 'see a GP first'; shaved head or a hat every day → suggest tablets; heavy stress with no family history → explain shedding and offer a blood test
- Ask the safety questions early (blood pressure, heart, mood, fertility plans, current medicines) and say in plain words when and why a product is ruled out, instead of a greyed-out card after sign-up
- Use photos and a clinician's eye to tell pattern loss from shedding, patchy or scarring loss, which a tick-box quiz cannot do
- Offer adjustable strengths and separate ingredients (for example a lower-strength topical finasteride, oral minoxidil in steps), so a side effect means changing one thing rather than the whole product
- Fill the gaps in Hims's range: dutasteride for men who do not respond to finasteride, medicated shampoo and scalp care, blood tests for common causes (iron, thyroid, vitamin D), and a clear option for men over 65
- Show real personalisation: let the patient see his own answers driving the plan, where Hims shows fixed 'Selected based on your goals' chips
- Price honestly: the monthly price up front, a true monthly plan, a reminder before any renewal price change, and self-service pause and cancel
- Treat men and women in one service with one account, and give women the same try-before-you-sign-up quiz that Hers UK lacks
- Build the plan around measured progress (standard photos at 0, 3 and 6 months) with a money-back promise for men too; Hers has a 180-day guarantee, Hims has none for hair

## Verification verdict
The part of this report that can be watched from outside is solid. The anonymous Hims quiz was mapped completely, every option was tested by stage 2, and an independent reviewer got the same result from fresh browsers: 11 questions, one fixed path, no stop, no warning, no product, no price, then an email wall; Hers has no anonymous step at all. Those findings carry the label 'observed' and each one names its run folder. The product catalogue is also reliable for names, forms and strengths (checked on live pages and in the app's served files), and reasonably reliable for prices, with the caveat that most Hims prices come from one embedded price table of the old shop. The part a competitor most wants, how medical answers decide which product is offered, could not be observed within the research rules. What the report says about it is honestly labelled: the mechanics (the server marks each product eligible or not, names one as the recommendation, greys out the rest, or rejects outright) are read from served code and are trustworthy as a description of the code; the content of the rules (which answer removes which product) rests on leaflets, help pages and reasoning, and should be treated as informed expectation, not fact. Read with those labels in mind, the report can be trusted. It is rated 'partial' only because the engine behind the wall remains unseen.

## Limitations
- The medical questions were not seen. Their wording and the answer-to-product rules live on the server and are sent only after sign-in, which the research rules forbid. Every rule about what happens after the wall comes from served code, Hims's own leaflets and help pages, or reasoning, and is labelled as such. None was watched happening.
- No recommendation was observed for any patient. It is not known which product Hims shows first for which answers, or whether the anonymous quiz answers influence it. They are attached to the account on sign-up, so they could.
- The one-change tests cover each of the 42 anonymous options on its own, one run with every answer changed, and two multi-select combinations (stage 2). Stage 3 repeated six of them and added contradictory answers, every warning-sign answer together, three more multi-select patterns, going back to change an answer, leaving and returning, and deleting answers. Other combinations were not tested; the served definition has no conditions, so none is expected to matter.
- Still not tested: coming back after several days (the first screen says answers are saved for 7 days), and anything that needs a sign-in. The phone layout was run twice.
- The old site's own experiment list names a 5% test called 'exp-2026-previous-hl-treatment'. Its bucket is set by the old site and was not sampled; nothing in the 41 browsers checked showed a different quiz.
- Nothing could be tested for women: Hers UK asks for a name on the first screen. Its questions are known only from the wording the app uses to play answers back, and the list of options is incomplete.
- Prices for Hims come from the price table the site embeds in its public pages and from 'from £…' marketing lines. That table belongs to the old shop system; the price at the new checkout is fetched after sign-in and could differ. Hers plan prices beyond 'from £13' and 'from £20' a month were not seen.
- Hims UK was part-way through moving platform on the day of the check (banner: 'We're moving to a new platform'). Findings about the old platform (the oral or topical preference question, the tag-based exclusions) are labelled as such and are not confirmed on the new one.
- Screens described from code (name, password, recommendation, options, plans, consent, GP details, rejection) were read as text in the app's files, not seen on a live page. Their order is partly inferred, and the code is shared with other brands and services on the same platform.
- Only part of the app's code was read (about 126 of 453 files). No archived copy of the old consultation could be reached (Wayback Machine, archive.ph and Common Crawl were all blocked from here); it may hold the old question set with a product exclusion on every option.
- Review quotes come from Trustpilot through a page-summary tool (read twice in stage 2 and twice more in stage 3, with the same score and the same quotes) and from the review panel on Hims's own pages; they are a small sample, not a survey. The tool gave the date of the 'Auto renewed at 43% more' review as 19 November 2025 in one reading and '2 days ago' in another, so its year is uncertain.
- Stage 3 checked product names, forms and 'from' prices on the live public pages, and strengths in the app's served product files and leaflet text. The full plan prices for Hims still rest on one source, the old shop's price table; six of the ten Hims products have no price on any public page.
- The leaflets of three compounded products (2-in-1 spray, both chews) no longer open at their own addresses: those addresses now redirect to the hair landing page. Their wording was read from the product data the site embeds in public pages, so it may be the old platform's text.
- Rules about what happens after the wall that rest on a leaflet's 'do not use' line say what Hims tells patients, not what the consultation does. The reviewer lowered their confidence to 'low' and relabelled the ones that only predict an engine action as 'inferred'.


---

# Hims (US), with sister brand Hers (US) (US) — digest
Slug: hims-us · Verified: yes · Audiences: men, women

Model: Subscription telehealth: online intake reviewed by a licensed provider (by message, or by video where a state requires it), then compounded or generic hair-loss medicines shipped on multi-month plans, plus over-the-counter products sold without a consultation.
Account wall: Reached at the state screen of the newer intake (https://www.hims.com/g/i/hl/select-state), after 14 anonymous screens: Continue stays disabled until the visitor ticks "I agree to the Terms and Conditions and Telehealth Consent". This research did not tick it, so the next screen was not seen. Signs that the account comes next: every screen has "Already have an account? Sign In", which goes to the old app (/c/hl?skipToSignIn=true&goFlow=true&flowId=hl), and the flow settings say legacyPath "/c/hl" and hold the date-of-birth rule (18 to 120) that the anonymous part never asks. The old app at /c/hl itself is blocked here (Cloudflare refuses its scripts).
Tested by: live-walk, site-code, public-docs
Coverage: Stage 1 mapped the catalogue and walked a landing-page pre-quiz. Stage 2 (experiment) found the newer anonymous intake at /g/i/hl, replayed its baseline, and ran 12 one-change replays plus 2 multi-change sweeps over the 11 hair questions (34 of 40 options seen live; stage 3 replayed the other 6, so all 40 are now seen) and 5 state replays (Texas, California, Massachusetts, Washington, AA, AP). Result: no hair answer changes anything; military mail states are refused; everything stops at the Terms and Telehealth Consent tick-box, which was not ticked. The pre-quiz was also tested (every option, no effect). NOT seen: the account step, date of birth, the medical form, photos, the recommendation, strengths, plan prices and any medical stop screen; the old /c/hl app stays blocked; Hers (women) stays blocked. The served flow data holds one screen at a time, not a full quiz definition, so no branch conditions could be read from code. Stage 3 (verify) replayed every baseline (all matched), re-tested the state rules (AA stop, Massachusetts valid), tested AE (stopped) and the 6 untested hair options (no change), and re-read 14 product pages and 2 safety pages.

Questions: 30 (18 before sign-in; 7 branch). Flows: lp-prequiz (3q), men-gi (15q), men (12q), women (0q)

## Engine summary
Hims US runs its hair consultation on a server-driven flow engine (flow-server.hims.com): the browser sends each answer and the server returns the next screen, one at a time. The anonymous part (hims.com/g/i/hl) is 11 questions about goals, where and how much hair is lost, when it started, hoped-for results, hair type and length, family history, stress and styling, with three education screens. It has no logic that a visitor can see: in 12 one-change replays and 2 sweeps, no answer added, removed or reworded a screen, raised a warning or named a product. Hims says so itself on screen one: the quiz "is not a medical assessment" and is not shared with a provider; the answers are kept with an anonymous session for personalisation. The first real gate is the state screen: armed-forces mail addresses (AA, AP) are refused, territories are not listed, and nobody can go on without agreeing to the Terms and the Telehealth Consent. After that (not seen, and the old /c/hl app that carries it is blocked here) Hims' public pages describe an account, date of birth (the served settings enforce 18 or over), a medical form built as a tree of follow-up questions, photos in most cases, and a pre-selected recommended product and dose that the customer may change, with plan length setting the price. The medical exclusions are published only as "who should not use" lists, and a licensed provider, not the software, makes the prescribing decision within about 48 hours.

## Rules
| id | when | then | effect | evidence | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Age under 18 | Not eligible for any prescription hair product. Published in the safety information ("are younger than 18 years of age"; "Anyone younger than 18 years of age"). How the questionnaire applies it was not seen. Code: the flow settings served by Hims' flow server set minAge 18 (R27). Code: the flow settings served by Hims' flow server set minAge 18 (R27). [Finasteride & Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Pill; Finasteride & Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew; Rx Hair Loss Spray (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray); Rx Hair Loss Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (4-in-1 spray); Rx Hair Loss Serum (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Serum); Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew; Finasteride (generic Propecia) 1 mg tablets] | hard-stop | docs | medium |
| R2 | Woman, or anyone pregnant, breastfeeding or able to become pregnant | Finasteride products are not for them: "intended for use by adult MEN ONLY" (spray); "intended for use by individuals assigned male at birth ... who are not pregnant, breastfeeding, or able to become pregnant" (finasteride, pill). Hims sends women to Hers. | Hers Spironolactone (for hair loss) | rx-oral | spironolactone not seen | tablet | women | US prescription only (off-label use) | unknown | Unknown here (Hers site not reachable from this machine). See the separate hers-us report. | docs |

## Who gets what
- Newer intake (/g/i/hl): baseline man (regrow, hairline, some loss, over a year, family history yes, sometimes stressed, short straight hair, no styling, Texas) → nothing [observed] No product is shown before the wall. The last anonymous screen is the state screen with the Terms and Telehealth Consent tick-box. The only product talk is generic: "Prescription treatments can help you ... grow visibly thicker, fuller hair in just 3 to 6 months."
- Newer intake: any one-change variant of the hair answers (receding or get ahead, top or all over, a lot or a little, recent onset, no family history, stressed all the time, no hair, buzzed, protective styles, 10+ minutes, and others) → nothing [observed] Identical to the baseline man: same screens, same end, no product (rule R21).
- Newer intake: man whose state is AA or AP (US armed forces mail) → nothing [observed] Stopped: "Currently, we don't offer this product in your state" (rule R23). Puerto Rico and other territories are not in the list at all (R24).
- Any visitor who finishes the landing-page pre-quiz (baseline: goals Both, simple solution Yes, prescription Yes; and each one-change variant) → Rx Hair Loss Spray (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray); Rx Hair Loss Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (4-in-1 spray) [observed] A sales pitch, not a recommendation: "Quick-drying, once-daily spray", "4-in-1 formula available with ketoconazole and biotin", buttons "Get the spray" and "See if treatment is right for me" → /c/hl. Identical for all five answer sets tested. No strength, plan or price is shown at this point.
- Visitor who answers "Not sure yet" to prescription treatment in the pre-quiz → Rx Hair Loss Spray (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray); Rx Hair Loss Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (4-in-1 spray) [observed] Same prescription spray pitch as everyone else; no non-prescription option is put forward (runs/exp-lp-04-q3-notsure).
- Baseline man in the real consultation: 32, hairline and crown, gradual, family history, no medical issues (NOT SEEN: consultation blocked) → Finasteride & Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Pill; Finasteride & Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew; Rx Hair Loss Spray (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray); Rx Hair Loss Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (4-in-1 spray); Rx Hair Loss Serum (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Serum); Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew; Finasteride (generic Propecia) 1 mg tablets; Finasteride & Minoxidil (two-product kit; probably the "Hair Loss Duo" named in Hims blog posts) [inferred] Not observed. Docs: a provider "recommends the right treatment for you" from the prescription range (pill, two chews, two sprays, serum, finasteride tablets, kit). Code: the site pre-selects one recommended product and dose that he may change. The default shown here (the spray) is inferred from a fl
- Man who wants no prescription, or only wants to buy a product → Minoxidil Foam 5%; Minoxidil Solution 5% ("Minoxidil Serum" / drops); Non-Prescription Hair Loss Kit; Thick Fix Thickening Shampoo; Thick Fix Thickening Conditioner; Biotin Gummies; Dandruff Detox Shampoo; Max Volume Shampoo; Max Volume Conditioner; Max Volume Shampoo & Conditioner [docs] Docs: these are sold by add to cart with no consultation (minoxidil foam and solution, non-prescription kit, shampoos, conditioner, gummies). No default is stated by Hims; foam is listed first here only for convenience.
- Man with a published exclusion for oral minoxidil + finasteride (for example certain heart disease, chronic kidney disease stage 3 to 5, blood thinners, liver disease) → Rx Hair Loss Spray (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Spray); Rx Hair Loss Spray + Ketoconazole + Biotin (4-in-1 spray); Rx Hair Loss Serum (Topical Finasteride & Minoxidil Serum); Finasteride (generic Propecia) 1 mg tablets [inferred] Inferred from the published "who should not take" list for the combination pill: the pill (and probably the chews, which also contain oral minoxidil) would be ruled out, leaving topical products or plain finasteride at the provider's discretion. Chronic liver disease or a history of breast or prosta
- Man already taking oral finasteride or dutasteride → Minoxidil + Supplement Blend Chew; Minoxidil Foam 5%; Minoxidil Solution 5% ("Minoxidil Serum" / drops) [inferred] Inferred from the safety pages: topical finasteride + minoxidil and the combination pill "cannot be used at the same time" as oral finasteride or another 5-alpha reductase inhibitor, which leaves minoxidil-only options. Not seen.
- Anyone under 18 → nothing [docs] Docs: every prescription hair product excludes people younger than 18. The stop screen was not seen.
- Baseline woman: 38, widening parting, not pregnant, no conditions → Hers Oral Minoxidil; Hers Minoxidil + hair vitamin ("Hair Blends", compounded); Hers Minoxidil + Biotin Chew; Hers Spironolactone (for hair loss) [docs] Hims US treats men only and sends women to Hers (forhers.com), which showed a security check on every page, so nothing was served or tested. The four Hers products are stubs from search snippets; which one Hers recommends is unknown here (see the separate hers-us report).

## Strengths
- Very wide format range for the same two actives (pill, two chews, two sprays, serum, plain finasteride tablets, kits, foam, solution), so there is nearly always a format to offer.
- Low headline price ("less than $1 a day", from $29 a month) tied to a 10-month plan paid upfront, which also locks the customer in.
- A recommended product and dose are pre-selected, but the customer can change product, strength and plan (page state); choice without a blank page.
- Free consultation, free shipping on prescriptions and unlimited provider messaging; providers in all 50 states with video visits where the law requires them.
- Over-the-counter products can be bought with no consultation, and prescriptions can be sent to a local pharmacy on request.
- Published safety information for each prescription product, with clear exclusion lists, and "Why We Ask" notes on intake questions.
- Heavy measurement: about 1,000 named experiments in the page, around 200 of them for hair, and every landing-page click is logged.
- A short, friendly anonymous quiz (14 screens, about 2 minutes) with plain words, pictures and "Choose as many as you'd like"; it builds commitment before asking for anything.
- Honest label on screen one: the quiz "is not a medical assessment" and is not shared with a provider.
- State check is live and immediate: an unsupported address gets a clear message before any account is made.

## Weaknesses
- The landing-page quiz is theatre: no answer changes the outcome, and a visitor who is "Not sure yet" about prescriptions is still pushed to the prescription spray. Yet the answers are logged against a device id.
- Headline prices differ between pages on the same day ($35 on /hair-loss, $29 on product and landing pages), and the low price needs a 10-month upfront payment. Plan prices are shown only inside the consultation.
- Most prescription products are compounded and not FDA-approved; the vitamin amounts in the chews are not published.
- Nothing public says which answers lead to which product: the customer cannot see why a product is recommended.
- Sudden or patchy hair loss is handled by a line in an FAQ ("seek medical attention"), not by anything visible before sign-up.
- After starting, hair customers cannot request a dose or product change through the self-service tool (weight-loss and sexual-health only); they must message the provider.
- Provider review can take 48 hours or more, and an order can sit on an unseen "action item".
- Women are sent to a separate site; the Hair Power Pack was out of stock.
- The site is hard to open from some networks: the consultation's own script files were refused by the security layer on every try.
- The 11-question anonymous intake has no clinical logic: diffuse loss, onset "in the past few months", "no" family history, constant stress, braids or locs, and "I don't have hair" all get the same screens and the same "prescription treatments can help" message.
- The education screen says "family history" and "stress" cause the loss even to a man who answered "No" family history or "Rarely" stressed: the copy is fixed, not personal.
- Answers are stored server-side with a session id before any consent or account, while the visitor is told they are not a medical assessment.
- No age, sex, pregnancy or medical question comes before the Terms and Telehealth Consent; a person who would be refused (under 18, a woman, a man with a contraindication) only finds out after signing up.

## Opportunities
- Make the quiz real and show it: tell the visitor which answer led to which product and strength, and what would change it.
- Publish one plain price table for every plan length before the quiz, with no upfront-payment footnote.
- Offer a true fork for people unsure about prescriptions: a non-prescription path (minoxidil 5%, ketoconazole shampoo) with a clear step-up later.
- Screen warning signs in the open before sign-up (sudden, patchy, scarring, scalp symptoms) and route them to in-person care; Hims does this only in an FAQ.
- Ask and act on things Hims does not appear to publish: finasteride side-effect worries and a low-dose or topical-only route, partner pregnancy plans, blood pressure for oral minoxidil.
- Offer licensed products and strength choice (for example dutasteride, or lower and higher topical strengths) that Hims does not sell; Hims has one topical strength pair plus the 4-in-1.
- Give short commitments (monthly, cancel any time) against Hims' 3 to 12 month plans.
- Let existing customers change dose or product themselves with a quick safety check, which Hims does not allow for hair.
- Take data only when needed: Hims logs marketing-quiz answers against a device id although it tells visitors the answers are not a medical assessment.
- Put the deal-breakers first: ask age, sex, current finasteride use and the main contraindications before asking for consent or an account, so nobody signs up to be refused.
- Make the anonymous answers matter: route sudden, diffuse or patchy loss, recent onset and traction styles (braids, locs) to a different path or an in-person check, which Hims does not do.
- Show a provisional recommendation and price before the account, with the reason ("because you said hairline, over a year, family history"); Hims shows neither.
- Serve armed-forces (APO/FPO) and territory customers, whom Hims turns away.

## Verification verdict
What is labelled observed holds up: every baseline replayed the same, every rule I re-tested live was confirmed, the six gaps in the anonymous quiz are now filled, and the product names, actives and strengths match the live pages. The main error found was prices: the public "starting at" price for the main prescription products dropped during the day, so the recorded $35 / $39 were out of date and have been corrected to $29 / $33. The report is trustworthy for the anonymous part of the Hims men's intake (which is marketing and data capture, with only a state-based stop) and for the public catalogue. It cannot tell you how Hims chooses a product or strength, because that happens after a consent box and an account, which this research does not cross; those parts are clearly labelled as docs, code-name hints or inference. The women's side (Hers) is essentially uncovered.

## Limitations
- The 12 remaining entries of the old "men" flow (after the wall) are topics from Hims' own articles and from field names, not screens. Their order, wording, options and "stage" (before or after the account wall) are guesses and are marked as such.
- Rules R1 to R6 and R14 to R19 are from published documents. They show what the provider must rule out and how review works, not what the software does with an answer. Rules R12, R13 and R20 rest on flag or field NAMES only and are low confidence.
- The default recommendation (the spray) is inferred, not seen.
- Plan-length prices are known only from footnotes, three Hims blog posts and one press review; two Hims blog posts contradict each other on the Duo kit. Hims runs price tests.
- The Hers (women) site was not seen at all; the four Hers products are stubs. See the separate hers-us report.
- Cached copies of the consultation script exist at the Internet Archive (per its availability service) but could not be opened from this machine; they would probably give the real hair-quiz questions.
- Web search stopped after one query in stage 2 (shared budget used up), so third-party walkthroughs, forum posts and videos were not searched again. One review that was fetched directly (Healthline) turned out to describe the erectile-dysfunction sign-up, not hair.
- The pre-quiz experiments used one landing page and a desktop browser; phone layout was not tested.
- The newer intake (/g/i/hl) was tested only up to the state screen: the Terms and Telehealth Consent tick-box was not ticked, so the account step, the medical form, any medical stop screen, the recommendation, strengths and plan prices were not seen.
- Not replayed (6 of 40 hair options): start "In the past year", expectations "More scalp coverage" and "Keep the hair I have" (two site errors), hair type "Textured or processed", length "Medium", style "I usually wear a hat". Marked inferred. Of the states, only Texas, California, Massachusetts, Washington, AA and AP were tried.
- Some options were seen only in multi-change sweeps (runs/exp-gi-14-all-different, runs/s2-gi-explore-2); a sweep cannot rule out two effects cancelling, though no screen differed at all.
- The flow server sends one screen at a time, so there is no full quiz definition to read. Its step numbers skip 1, 3, 7 and 8, which may be screens shown to other visitors (tests, other entry points); none appeared in any replay.
- Desktop browser, locale en-US, timezone America/New_York, one day (23 Nov 2026). Hims runs many experiments, so another visitor may see a different version.
- Stage 3: prices on the public pages changed during the day of research ($35 to $29 for the main Rx products, $39 to $33 for the 4-in-1 spray). Treat any public price here as a snapshot.


---

# Keeps (US) — digest
Slug: keeps · Verified: yes · Audiences: men

Model: US men's telehealth subscription for male pattern hair loss: a short marketing quiz names a compounded prescription product, the patient pays, and a licensed provider reviews a medical intake afterwards.
Account wall: Main and Gel funnels: /express/checkout, reached from the plan page button 'Continue to checkout' (one page asking name, address, phone, date of birth, email and card). Legacy quiz: /quiz/lead (name, email, phone) straight after the last question. Request flow: /request/lead (name, email) on the first screen. The medical intake (/consultation/intake) redirects to sign-in.
Tested by: live-walk, site-code, public-docs
Coverage: Stage 1 read all three quizzes in served JavaScript. Stage 2 replayed all three baselines and ran 41 one-change experiments (19 main quiz, 3 gel, 19 legacy) to the plan page or account wall. Every product-changing rule read in the code was confirmed live. Not tested: the remaining 'no effect' options of the main quiz one by one (code shows no branch condition, 13 replays agreed), most of the 51 accepted states, the medical intake after payment and sign-in, and the clinician's decision. Keeps does not treat women (FAQ; tick-box gate). Stage 3 (independent verify) replayed all three baselines fresh, re-tested 9 high-impact rules live (all confirmed), ran 5 new gap replays and re-read 10 product pages plus the live price data.

Questions: 63 (51 before sign-in; 20 branch). Flows: men (26q), men-gel (25q), men-legacy (9q), men-plan-selection (1q), men-request (2q)

## Engine summary
Before payment the main Keeps quiz is a two-answer lookup, now proved live. 'How would you like to treat your hair loss?' picks the family (Topical Drop gives Drop/Drop+, anything else gives Chew/Chew+) and 'What's your top priority?' picks the strength ('simple & affordable' gives the finasteride product, anything else the dutasteride 'plus' product). Thirteen other one-change replays (onset under 6 months, advanced loss, patchy loss, earlier side effects, goal, state) changed nothing but the echoed label. The only stops are a 'Mostly Scalp' picture, twelve refused states and an unticked '18+ and assigned male at birth' box; women cannot pass the tick box (the FAQ says the service is only for people assigned male at birth). The Gel funnel always names Gel+. The older /quiz is a real rule table, also proved live: location and pill preference choose finasteride, foam or oral minoxidil; tretinoin or vitamins swap foam for a compounded spray; dandruff, ketoconazole, saw palmetto, hair care and pomade each add one product; patchy loss goes to a clinician request. In every flow the medical screening happens after the account wall and payment.

## Rules
| id | when | then | effect | evidence | conf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | men-form = 'Topical Drop (Rx Serum)' | The topical family is recommended (Drop+ by default, Drop with the affordable priority); any other answer, including 'not sure', gives the oral family. [Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R2 | men-priority = 'Keeping treatment simple & affordable' | The cheaper finasteride product is recommended (Chew or Drop); every other answer gives the dutasteride 'plus' product (Chew+ or Drop+). [Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R3 | second picture answer = 'Mostly Scalp' | The quiz stops with 'This treatment may not be a good fit right now ... prescription hair loss treatments are unlikely to be effective at this stage.' Only 'Back to homepage' is offered. Patchy Loss, Diffuse Thinning and 'Advanced' do not stop. [Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil] | hard-stop | observed | high |
| R4 | men-state is one of AA, AE, AP, DC, DE, ID, KS, MS, NM, PR, RI, WV | The page shows 'We currently don't serve in your state. Please check back soon.' and will not continue. [Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Gel+ 10-In-1: Dutasteride, Finasteride & Minoxidil] | hard-stop | observed | high |
| R5 | the '18+ and assigned male at birth' box is not ticked | The 'Show my plan' button stays disabled. | hard-stop | observed | high |
| R6 | any other anonymous answer (goal, stage, picture, duration, comments, treated before, how it went) | No change to the recommended product or price; the answers are only stored in the browser and repeated on screen. | none | observed | high |
| R7 | any answers | Gel+ (product id 19267) is always recommended. [Gel+ 10-In-1: Dutasteride, Finasteride & Minoxidil] | none | observed | high |
| R8 | leg-where = 'Patchy (coin-sized bald spots)' | No recommendation; the browser is sent to the clinician-request flow. | flag-for-clinician | observed | high |
| R9 | leg-where = 'Nowhere yet...' | Finasteride is left out; the family-history question replaces the expectation and pill-preference questions; minoxidil foam plus thickening shampoo and conditioner are recommended. [Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Minoxidil Foam, 5%; Keeps Thickening Shampoo; Keeps Thickening Conditioner] | product-removed | observed | high |
| R10 | leg-form = 'Pills (taken orally)' and location is hairline, crown or both; or location = hairline with no preference | Oral minoxidil 2.5 mg is added (and topical minoxidil is dropped when pills are preferred). [Minoxidil Foam, 5%; Oral Minoxidil 2.5 mg tablets; Minoxidil+ Spray] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R11 | leg-dandruff = 'Yes' or 'Products with ketoconazole' ticked | Ketoconazole 2% shampoo is added. [Ketoconazole Shampoo, 2%] | product-added | observed | high |
| R12 | location = crown or 'a bit of both', no pill preference, no ingredient or OTC interest, no dandruff | Finasteride 1 mg (id 211) plus minoxidil 5% foam (id 239), both 3-month. [Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Minoxidil Foam, 5%] | product-added | observed | high |
| R13 | gel-priority = 'Keeping treatment simple & affordable' (or any other answer) | No change: Gel+ (19267) is still the only product shown. [Gel+ 10-In-1: Dutasteride, Finasteride & Minoxidil] | none | observed | high |
| R14 | leg-where = 'Hairline' and leg-form = 'Topicals' | Finasteride 1 mg alone; no minoxidil of any kind (oral is excluded by the preference, foam is never offered for hairline-only). [Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Minoxidil Foam, 5%; Oral Minoxidil 2.5 mg tablets] | product-removed | observed | high |
| R15 | 'Products with tretinoin' ticked (leg-ingredients) | Minoxidil 5% foam is swapped for the compounded Minoxidil+ Spray (11840). [Minoxidil Foam, 5%; Minoxidil+ Spray] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R16 | 'Vitamins and supplements' ticked (leg-otc) | Daily Hair Defense Supplement (2874) is added AND minoxidil foam is swapped for Minoxidil+ Spray (11840). [Minoxidil Foam, 5%; Minoxidil+ Spray; Daily Hair Defense Supplement] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R17 | 'Products with saw palmetto' ticked (leg-ingredients) | Daily Hair Defense Supplement (2874) is added; nothing else changes. [Daily Hair Defense Supplement] | product-added | observed | high |
| R18 | 'Hair care products (shampoo and conditioner)' ticked (leg-otc) | Thickening shampoo (499) and conditioner (500) are added. [Keeps Thickening Shampoo; Keeps Thickening Conditioner] | product-added | observed | high |
| R19 | 'Hair styling products (pomade)' ticked (leg-otc) | Thickening pomade (682) is added. [Thickening Pomade] | product-added | observed | high |
| R20 | leg-expect = any option, or 'Products with finasteride' / 'Products with minoxidil' ticked | No change to the basket (finasteride + foam for the baseline). | none | observed | high |
| R21 | men-treated = 'Yes' | Adds 'How did it go?'. No answer to it (including 'Had side effects') changes the product, adds a warning or asks what the side effect was. | extra-questions | observed | high |
| R22 | plan page: 'Want to switch to topical solutions?' clicked | Drop and Drop+ cards replace Chew and Chew+, each with its own price; the 'Recommended for you: Chew+' banner stays. [Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R23 | gel plan page: 'Want to switch to oral solutions?' clicked | Chew and Chew+ cards are shown instead of Gel+. [Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil] | product-switched | observed | high |
| R24 | Patient is a woman (no question asks sex; the only check is the '18+ and assigned male at birth' tick box) | Women cannot continue: without the tick 'Show my plan' stays disabled. The FAQ says only people assigned male at birth can use Keeps. A separate page /not-eligible-user/sex says 'We're sorry Keeps can't help you, but Nurx can', but the hair quizzes do not send anyone there (in the served code it is only named in the route list of the app bundle). | hard-stop | observed | high |
| R25 | leg-form = 'Pills (taken orally)' together with 'Products with tretinoin' (or 'Vitamins and supplements') | The pill preference wins: finasteride + oral minoxidil (211, 989). The Minoxidil+ Spray swap (R15/R16) does not happen, because the spray is only offered when no pill preference is given. [Minoxidil+ Spray; Oral Minoxidil 2.5 mg tablets] | product-removed | observed | high |
| R26 | gel-treated = 'Yes' | Adds 'How did it go?'. 'Had side effects' gives no warning or follow-up and Gel+ is still recommended. | extra-questions | observed | high |

## Who gets what
- Man, 32, receding hairline + crown ('Patterned', picture 'Moderate'), 1-3 years, never treated, wants regrowth, no preference on form, priority 'Balanced results', Texas (main funnel) → Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil [observed] Plan page says 'Good news - you're approved' and 'Chew+ is clinically recommended'. Chew is the second card; Drop and Drop+ are behind 'Want to switch to topical solutions?'. Proof: runs/baseline-men/17.txt; replayed runs/baseline-men-r2. Checkout: $100.00 today, then $204.99 every 3 months (3-month
- Same man in the Gel funnel → Gel+ 10-In-1: Dutasteride, Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil [observed] Only Gel+ is shown; 'Want to switch to oral solutions?' reveals Chew and Chew+. Proof: runs/baseline-men-gel/16.txt.
- Same man in the legacy mini quiz ('A bit of both', regrow, no preference, no dandruff) → Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Minoxidil Foam, 5% [observed] Product ids 211 and 239 appear in the address of the account-wall page; the plan itself is not shown without an account. Proof: runs/baseline-men-legacy-quiz/10.txt.
- Baseline woman (38, diffuse thinning) → nothing [observed] Not served. There is no sex question; the '18+ and assigned male at birth' tick box keeps 'Show my plan' disabled (runs/verify-R5-notick). FAQ: 'only those assigned male at birth can use Keeps'. A /not-eligible-user/sex page refers women to Nurx, but the quiz does not route there.
- Baseline man, priority 'Keeping treatment simple & affordable' → Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil [observed] Chew (finasteride 1 mg + minoxidil 2.5 mg + D3). Checkout $94.96, then $159.99 per 3 months. runs/exp-02.
- Baseline man, form 'Topical Drop (Rx Serum)' → Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil [observed] Drop+ (topical dutasteride + minoxidil). Checkout $149.93, then $214.99 per 3 months. runs/exp-01.

Checkout $140.02, then $194.99 per 3 months. runs/exp-03.

Proved live for Idaho (runs/exp-09), West Virginia (runs/verify-R4-wv) and Delaware (runs/verify-R4-de); the other nine codes come from the served code list.
- Man with side effects from earlier treatment, patchy loss, loss under 6 months or advanced loss (main quiz) → Chew+ 5-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil; Chew 3-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop 4-In-1: Finasteride & Minoxidil; Drop+ 11-In-1: Dutasteride & Minoxidil [observed] Exactly the baseline offer (Chew+): these answers are only echoed back. runs/exp-08, 11, 12, 13, 15, 18, 19.
- Legacy quiz: hairline only, no preference or pills → Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Oral Minoxidil 2.5 mg tablets [observed] Finasteride 1 mg + oral minoxidil 2.5 mg. runs/exp-L01, exp-L19.
- Legacy quiz: crown or both, prefers pills → Finasteride 1 mg tablets; Oral Minoxidil 2.5 mg tablets [observed] Finasteride + oral minoxidil; foam dropped. runs/exp-L05.

- Legacy quiz: patchy (coin-sized) bald spots → nothing [observed] No basket: sent to the clinician request form /request/lead (name and email wall). runs/exp-L03.

## Strengths
- Very short path: about ten taps and no typing to reach a named product and a price.
- Patients steer the offer with two plain choices (oral or topical; strongest or cheapest), and switch links on the plan page show the other family at once.
- An honest early stop for advanced baldness ('Mostly Scalp'): it tells the patient prescription treatment is unlikely to work and points to restoration or cosmetic options.
- Clear price ladder with a large first-order discount and a 180-day money-back guarantee on the new compounded range.
- Wide range: licensed generics (finasteride, minoxidil, ketoconazole), compounded combinations (oral and topical, finasteride or dutasteride) and cosmetic add-ons.
- Honest legal labelling on product pages: compounded products are marked 'not FDA-approved'.

## Weaknesses
- 'You're approved' and 'clinically recommended' are shown before any medical question; the loaders ('Confirming clinical eligibility') are timed animations (about 5 seconds, set in served chunk 6717).
- Proved live: 'Had side effects' from earlier treatment, onset under 6 months and patchy loss change nothing, give no warning and ask no follow-up (runs/exp-08, 11, 12).
- The default for every man who does not ask for cheap is a compounded dutasteride product (off-label for hair loss in the US), never licensed finasteride first.
- Payment is taken before the medical intake; a product page says 'You won't be charged until your prescription is approved' while the checkout says the card is charged and refunded if not approved.
- Age and sex are checked with one tick box; no women's service at all (referred to Nurx).
- Renewal price ($204.99 per 3 months for Chew+) is about double the first order ($100) and appears only in the checkout small print.
- Two older funnels (legacy /quiz and /plan-selection) still run with different logic and products, so the same man can be sent to different treatments depending on the entry page.

## Opportunities
- Ask the real safety questions before payment (heart, blood pressure, fertility plans, mood, earlier side effects) and let them visibly steer the offer, then say 'likely suitable, a clinician will confirm' instead of 'approved'.
- Start with licensed finasteride and step up to dutasteride only on a reason (no response, intolerance) and say why.
- Triage patchy, sudden or recent loss and scalp symptoms (redness, burning, scaling) to a clinician instead of passing them silently.
- Serve women: Keeps sends them away; a combined men-and-women service with a proper female pathway (pregnancy, contraception, PCOS) is an open gap.
- Show the renewal price next to the first-order price.

## Verification verdict
Good trust for what happens before the account wall. Every high-impact rule I re-tested (9 rules, 13 live runs plus code re-reads of the current served chunks) came out as recorded, the three baselines replayed to the same product and price, and 16 product price and strength facts matched the live price data. The main fault found was the women's route: the report said women are referred to Nurx, but the quiz never routes there; women are simply blocked by the tick box and told by the FAQ that Keeps is for people assigned male at birth. A few labels were tightened. The report cannot say anything reliable about the medical intake or the clinician's decisions, which sit behind payment and sign-in, and prices are time-limited promotions.

## Limitations
- The medical questionnaire, photo step and provider decision sit behind payment and sign-in; none of it was seen. Its question wording is unknown and its purpose is described from code labels and FAQ text only.
- Not every 'no effect' option was replayed one by one. Still not replayed: stages 'Visible' and 'Not sure', picture 'Mild', second picture 'Receding Hairline', durations '6-12 months', '3+ years' and 'I'm not sure', comments 'I'm not sure', 'How did it go?' answers 'Saw some results' and 'Stopped too early', and most gel options. The served code gives them no branch condition and 17 sampled replays (13 in stage 2, 4 in stage 3) agreed.
- At the state gate Idaho, West Virginia and Delaware (refused) and California, Texas and Arkansas (accepted) were replayed; the other refused codes come from the served code list. Kansas itself was not replayed (typing 'Kansas' selects Arkansas first).
- Legacy quiz: only the family option 'I'm not sure' was replayed, and multi-selects were tested one option at a time except one combination (pills + tretinoin, runs/verify-gap-leg-pills-tret). Other combinations rest on the served rule table.
- Prices are first-order promotional prices seen on 2026-11-23 during a 'Black Friday / up to 50% off' offer (offer id 1040); they will change.
- Feature flags change what a visitor sees (for example the phone-number screen was off and the free-gift offer variant was not served). Another visitor may get a different variant.
- Whether the provider ever changes the quiz product (for example to a lower dutasteride dose or to finasteride) cannot be seen without an account.
- The /product-info and /drug-facts pages were linked from product pages but not read in full.


---

# LloydsPharmacy Online Doctor (UK) — digest
Slug: lloyds-online-doctor · Verified: yes · Audiences: men

Model: UK online pharmacy and doctor service (Shopify shop front plus a server-driven questionnaire engine) selling licensed and off-label prescription hair-loss medicines to men.
Account wall: No sign-in is needed for the questions or the recommendation. "Continue as a new patient" (…/clinic/hair-loss?guest) starts as a guest. After the recommendation, choosing a product, the pack size (Add to basket) and a finasteride safety screen, the last screen is "Please confirm the following to complete your consultation" (question 100467, "I confirm the above"). The walk stopped there and did not press it, so the exact sign-in point (expected straight after it) was not seen.
Tested by: live-walk, site-code, public-docs
Coverage: Stage 1 walked every question on the baseline path and every option one level deep. Stage 2 (24 Nov 2026) replayed the baseline and ran 33 one-change experiments to the recommendation screen, comparing cards and wording (runs/e01 to runs/e33). Options not replayed are marked unknown or inferred. Stage 3 (24 Nov 2026) replayed the baseline and 8 key rules (all confirmed) and filled 7 gaps (phaeochromocytoma, porphyria, SLE, scalp creams, eGFR not tested, testosterone, and one two-change combination); see verification.

Questions: 53 (53 before sign-in; 28 branch). Flows: men (53q)

## Engine summary
A server-side rules engine (one screen per answer) works as a subtraction filter. It starts from six cards for every eligible man (two combination packs, generic finasteride, oral minoxidil, Sons minoxidil spray, Propecia) and removes whole drug families: any finasteride exclusion (baby plans, breast or prostate cancer, breast tissue enlargement, sex-life problems, 5-ARI or ED medicines, allergy, mood opt-out, past side effects declined) drops every finasteride product; heart/BP disease, BP medicines or minoxidil allergy drop every minoxidil product, even the spray; age 65+, kidney disease, phaeochromocytoma, porphyria, SLE or ED tablets drop oral minoxidil; scalp changes or medicated scalp creams drop topicals. When both families are gone, the questions end early with no product. Hard stops end the questions for women, under-18s, non-pattern loss and severe kidney or liver disease. Answers never change strength, dose, plan or price, and never add a product: history and efficacy answers only change the result wording. Follow-up questions are skipped once the drug they protect is gone.
